A_{1}\sqcup A_{2}
with
A_{1}\cup A_{2}
, and we may write the disjoint union topology simply as
\{U_{1}\cup U_{2}:U_{1}\in A_{1}\cap\tau,U_{2}\in A_{2}\cap\tau\}
.

Lemma 34
.

Let
\mathcal{Z}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{Z}_{d}
be topological spaces, and let
\mathcal{Z}\coloneqq\prod_{j=1}^{d}\mathcal{Z}_{j}
be the product space equipped with the product topology. Let
S\subseteq[d]
be non-empty and let
\mathcal{Z}_{S}\coloneqq\prod_{j\in S}\mathcal{Z}_{j}
be the product space equipped with the product topology.

(a)

If
U\subseteq\mathcal{Z}
is open, then the set
U_{S}\coloneqq\{x_{S}:x\in U\}
is open in
\mathcal{Z}_{S}
.

(b)

If
K\subseteq\mathcal{Z}
is compact, then the set
K_{S}\coloneqq\{x_{S}:x\in K\}
is compact in
\mathcal{Z}_{S}
.

Proof.

(a)
We can write
U=\bigcup_{i\in I}U^{(i)}
for some index set
I
, where
U^{(i)}=\prod_{j=1}^{d}U^{(i)}_{j}
and
U^{(i)}_{j}
is open in
\mathcal{Z}_{j}
for all
i\in I
,
j\in[d]
. Hence
U_{S}=\bigcup_{i\in I}U^{(i)}_{S}
where
U^{(i)}_{S}=\prod_{j\in S}U^{(i)}_{j}
, so
U_{S}
is open in
\mathcal{Z}_{S}
.

(b)
For any open cover
\{U^{(i)}_{S}\}_{i\in I}
of
K_{S}
, define
U^{(i)}\coloneqq\{x\in\mathcal{Z}:x_{S}\in U^{(i)}_{S}\}
for
i\in I
. Note that
U^{(i)}
is open in
\mathcal{Z}
for
i\in I
, as it is the pre-image of an open set under a projection map (which is continuous, by definition of the product topology). Thus,
\{U^{(i)}\}_{i\in I}
is an open cover of
K
, which has a finite subcover
I_{0}\subseteq I
since
K
is compact. Therefore,
\{U^{(i)}_{S}\}_{i\in I_{0}}
is also a finite subcover of
K_{S}
, so
K_{S}
is compact in
\mathcal{Z}_{S}
.
∎

Lemma 35
.

Let
\mathcal{X}_{1}
and
\mathcal{X}_{2}
be topological spaces.

(a)

If
\mathcal{X}_{1}
and
\mathcal{X}_{2}
are Hausdorff, then
\mathcal{X}_{1}\times\mathcal{X}_{2}
is Hausdorff in the product topology and
\mathcal{X}_{1}\sqcup\mathcal{X}_{2}
is Hausdorff in the disjoint union topology.

(b)

If
\mathcal{X}_{1}
and
\mathcal{X}_{2}
are locally compact, then
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{X}_{2}
is locally compact in the product topology and
\mathcal{X}_{1}\sqcup\mathcal{X}_{2}
is locally compact in the disjoint union topology.

Proof.

(a)
The first statement follows from
Munkres 2014
.
For the second statement, let
(x_{1},j_{1}),(x_{2},j_{2})\in\mathcal{X}_{1}\sqcup\mathcal{X}_{2}
be distinct, where
j_{1},j_{2}\in\{1,2\}
, and for
\ell\in\{1,2\}
, we have
x_{\ell}\in\mathcal{X}_{\ell}
. If
j_{1}=j_{2}
, then the result follows from the fact that
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{X}_{2}
are Hausdorff. Otherwise, we can separate the two points using the open sets
\mathcal{X}_{1}\times\{1\}
and
\mathcal{X}_{2}\times\{2\}
.

(b)
For the first statement, if
x_{1}\in\mathcal{X}_{1}
and
x_{2}\in\mathcal{X}_{2}
, then we can find compact neighbourhoods
K_{j}\subseteq\mathcal{X}_{j}
of
x_{j}
for
j\in\{1,2\}
. Then by Tychonov’s theorem
(
Munkres 2014
, Theorem 37.3)
,
K_{1}\times K_{2}
is a compact neighbourhood of
(x_{1},x_{2})
. For the second statement, if
(x,j)\in\mathcal{X}_{1}\sqcup\mathcal{X}_{2}
, then we can find a compact subset
K\subseteq\mathcal{X}_{j}
containing
x
. Then
K\times\{j\}
is a compact subset of
\mathcal{X}_{1}\sqcup\mathcal{X}_{2}
containing
(x,j)
.
∎

Lemma 36
.

Let
\mathcal{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{X}_{d}
be locally compact Hausdorff spaces, and let
\mathcal{X}\coloneqq\prod_{j=1}^{d}\mathcal{X}_{j}
. Then
\mathcal{X}
,
\mathcal{X}_{\star}
and
\mathcal{X}\times 2^{[d]}
are locally compact Hausdorff spaces. Moreover, if every open set in
\mathcal{X}
is
\sigma
-compact, then
\mathcal{X}_{\star}
and
\mathcal{X}\times 2^{[d]}
also have this property.

Proof.

The fact that
\mathcal{X}
is a locally compact Hausdorff space follows from Lemma
35
. Moreover, the singleton space
\{\star\}
as well as the space
2^{[d]}
endowed with the discrete topology are both locally compact Hausdorff spaces. We observe that
\mathcal{X}
,
\mathcal{X}_{\star}
and
\mathcal{X}\times 2^{[d]}
can be generated from
\mathcal{X}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{X}_{d}
,
\{\star\}
,
2^{[d]}
via a combination of product space and disjoint union operations. Hence the first result follows from Lemma
35
.

To check that every open set in
\mathcal{X}_{\star}=\bigsqcup_{S\in 2^{[d]}}\mathcal{X}^{(S)}
is
\sigma
-compact, observe that for any open set
U\subseteq\mathcal{X}_{\star}
, we can write
U=\bigcup_{S\subseteq[d]}U^{(S)}
where
U^{(S)}\coloneqq U\cap\mathcal{X}^{(S)}
. Therefore, it suffices to show that for every
S\subseteq[d]
, any open set
U\subseteq\mathcal{X}^{(S)}
is
\sigma
-compact. Let
U_{S}\coloneqq\{a_{S}:a\in U\}
and let
V\coloneqq\{x\in\mathcal{X}:x_{S}\in U_{S}\}
. Then
V
is open in
\mathcal{X}
since it is the pre-image of a projection of an open set, so we can write
V=\bigcup_{i=1}^{\infty}K(i)
, where
K(i)
is compact in
\mathcal{X}
for each
i
. Moreover,
U_{S}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{\infty}K(i)_{S}
, and
K(i)_{S}
is compact in
\mathcal{X}_{S}
by Lemma
34
(b)
. We claim that
K(i)^{(S)}\coloneqq\{z\in\mathcal{X}_{\star}:z_{S}\in K(i)_{S},\,z_{j}=\star\;\forall j\notin S\}
is compact. To see this, consider any open cover
\{U(j)\}_{j\in J}
of
K(i)^{(S)}
, where, without loss of generality, we assume that
U(j)\subseteq\mathcal{X}^{(S)}
for all
j\in J
. Writing
U(j)_{S}\coloneqq\{a_{S}:a\in U(j)\}
for
j\in J
, we have by Lemma
34
(a)
that
\{U(j)_{S}\}_{j\in J}
forms an open cover of
K(i)_{S}
. We can therefore find a finite subcover
\{U(j)_{S}\}_{j\in J_{0}}
of
K(i)_{S}
, so that
\{U(j)\}_{j\in J_{0}}
forms a finite subcover of
K(i)^{(S)}
. We deduce that
U=\bigcup_{i=1}^{\infty}K(i)^{(S)}
is a countable union of compact sets.

To show that every open set in
\mathcal{X}\times 2^{[d]}
is
\sigma
-compact, observe that since
2^{[d]}
is finite, any open set in
\mathcal{X}\times 2^{[d]}
is of the form
\bigcup_{S\subseteq[d]}\bigl\{U(S)\times S\bigr\}
, where
U(S)
is open in
\mathcal{X}
. Since each
U(S)
is
\sigma
-compact and
S
is finite (hence compact), it follows that each set
U(S)\times S
is
\sigma
-compact, and hence
\bigcup_{S\subseteq[d]}\bigl\{U(S)\times S\bigr\}
is
\sigma
-compact.
∎

Lemma 37
.

If
(\mathcal{X}_{1},\tau_{1}),\ldots,(\mathcal{X}_{d},\tau_{d})
are Polish spaces, then the Cartesian product space
\mathcal{X}_{\star}\coloneqq\prod_{j=1}^{d}\mathcal{X}_{j,\star}
equipped with the product topology is also a Polish space.

Proof.

A finite (or even countable) Cartesian product of Polish spaces is Polish
(
Kechris 2012
, Proposition 3.3)
, so it suffices to show that
(\mathcal{X}_{j,\star},\tau_{j,\star})
is Polish for each
j\in[d]
, where
\tau_{j,\star}\coloneqq\tau_{j}\cup\{A\cup\{\star\}:A\in\tau_{j}\}
. Now fix
j\in[d]
, and find a countable dense subset
\{x_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
of
\mathcal{X}_{j}
. Then
\{\star\}\cup\{x_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
is a countable dense subset of
\mathcal{X}_{j,\star}
, so
\mathcal{X}_{j,\star}
is separable. Now find a metric
d
on
\mathcal{X}_{j}
such that
d
generates the topology
\tau_{j}
and
(\mathcal{X}_{j},d)
is complete. Define the standard bounded metric
\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\hskip 2.08334pt\hskip 0.0pt\rule[8.23611pt]{2.96542pt}{0.43057pt}}{d}
by
\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\hskip 2.08334pt\hskip 0.0pt\rule[8.23611pt]{2.96542pt}{0.43057pt}}{d}(x,y)\coloneqq d(x,y)\wedge 1
for
x,y\in\mathcal{X}_{j}
. Then, by
Munkres 2014
,
\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\hskip 2.08334pt\hskip 0.0pt\rule[8.23611pt]{2.96542pt}{0.43057pt}}{d}
also generates the topology
\tau_{j}
. Define a metric
d^{\prime}
on
\mathcal{X}_{j,\star}
by
d^{\prime}(x,y)\coloneqq\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\hskip 2.08334pt\hskip 0.0pt\rule[8.23611pt]{2.96542pt}{0.43057pt}}{d}(x,y)
for
x,y\in\mathcal{X}_{j}
,
d^{\prime}(x,\star)\coloneqq 2
for
x\in\mathcal{X}_{j}
, and
d^{\prime}(\star,\star)\coloneqq 0
. Letting
\tau_{j,\star}^{\prime}
denote the topology on
\mathcal{X}_{j,\star}
generated by
d^{\prime}
, we first show that
\tau_{j,\star}^{\prime}=\tau_{j,\star}
. On the one hand, since
\{\star\}\in\tau_{j,\star}^{\prime}
and
\tau_{j}\subseteq\tau_{j,\star}^{\prime}
, we have
\tau_{j,\star}\subseteq\tau_{j,\star}^{\prime}
. On the other hand, if
x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}_{j,\star}
,
r\geq 0
and
A\coloneqq\{x\in\mathcal{X}_{j,\star}:d^{\prime}(x,x_{0})<r\}
denotes an open ball in
\tau_{j,\star}^{\prime}
, then

A=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\mathcal{X}_{j,\star}&\mbox{if $r>2$}\\
\{x\in\mathcal{X}_{j}:\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\hskip 2.08334pt\hskip 0.0pt\rule[8.23611pt]{2.96542pt}{0.43057pt}}{d}(x,x_{0})<r\}&\mbox{if $r\leq 2$ and $x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}_{j}$}\\
\{\star\}&\mbox{if $r\leq 2$ and $x_{0}=\star$.}\end{array}\right.

We deduce that
A\in\tau_{j,\star}
, so since such open balls generate
\tau_{j,\star}^{\prime}
, we have
\tau_{j,\star}^{\prime}\subseteq\tau_{j,\star}
. Hence,
d^{\prime}
generates the topology
\tau_{j,\star}
. Next, we show that
(\mathcal{X}_{j,\star},d^{\prime})
is complete. Let
(z_{n})_{n=1}^{\infty}
be a Cauchy sequence in
\mathcal{X}_{j,\star}
, so there exists
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that
d^{\prime}(z_{n_{1}},z_{n_{2}})\leq 1/2
for all
n_{1},n_{2}\geq N
. Therefore, either
z_{n}=\star
for all
n\geq N
or
z_{n}\in\mathcal{X}_{j}
for all
n\geq N
. In the former case,
z_{n}\to\star
as
n\rightarrow\infty
. In the latter case,
(z_{n})_{n=N}^{\infty}
is also a Cauchy sequence in
(\mathcal{X}_{j},d)
and hence by completeness of
(\mathcal{X}_{j},d)
, it has a limit in
\mathcal{X}_{j}
. This shows that
(\mathcal{X}_{j,\star},d^{\prime})
is complete and
d^{\prime}
generates the topology
\tau_{j,\star}
, so
(\mathcal{X}_{j,\star},\tau_{j,\star})
is completely metrisable. Therefore,
(\mathcal{X}_{j,\star},\tau_{j,\star})
is a Polish space, as required.
∎

Lemma 38
.

Suppose that
(B_{i})_{i\in[n]}\overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}\mathsf{Ber}(q)
.

(a)

With probability at least
1-\delta
,

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}B_{i}\leq 2q+\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{n}.

(b)

If
q\geq\frac{8\log(1/\delta)}{n}
, then with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}B_{i}\geq\frac{q}{2}.

Proof.

(a)
By Bernstein’s inequality
(
Boucheron, Lugosi and Massart 2013
, Theorem 2.10)
, we have with probability at least
1-\delta
that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}B_{i}

\displaystyle\leq q+\sqrt{\frac{2q(1-q)}{n}}\log^{1/2}(1/\delta)+\frac{1}{3n}\log(1/\delta)

\displaystyle\leq\biggl(q^{1/2}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2n}}\log^{1/2}(1/\delta)\biggr)^{2}\leq 2q+\frac{1}{n}\log(1/\delta).

(b)
By the multiplicative Chernoff bound
(
McDiarmid 1998
, Theorem 2.3(c))
for the sum of independent Bernoulli random variables, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\biggl(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}B_{i}\leq\frac{q}{2}\biggr)\leq\exp(-nq/8)\leq\delta,

where the final inequality follows from the assumption that
q\geq\frac{8\log(1/\delta)}{n}
.
∎

Lemma 39
.

Let
0<r_{1}\leq r_{2}
. Then
\mathcal{P}_{\psi_{r_{2}}}(\theta_{0},\sigma^{2})\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{\psi_{r_{1}}}(\theta_{0},\sigma^{2})
.

Proof.

Let
X\sim P\in\mathcal{P}_{\psi_{r_{2}}}(\theta_{0},\sigma^{2})
. Then

\displaystyle 2\geq\mathbb{E}\biggl\{\exp\biggl(\frac{|X-\theta_{0}|^{r_{2}}}{\sigma^{r_{2}}}\biggr)\biggr\}\geq\biggl[\mathbb{E}\biggl\{\exp\biggl(\frac{|X-\theta_{0}|^{r_{1}}}{\sigma^{r_{1}}}\biggr)\biggr\}\biggr]^{r_{2}/r_{1}},

by Jensen’s inequality. Thus
\mathbb{E}\exp\bigl(|X-\theta_{0}|^{r_{1}}/\sigma^{r_{1}}\bigr)\leq 2
, so
P\in\mathcal{P}_{\psi_{r_{1}}}(\theta_{0},\sigma^{2})
.
∎

Lemma 40
.

Let
r>1
,
\sigma>0
and
X\sim P\in\mathcal{P}_{\psi_{r}}(0,\sigma^{2})
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\exp(\lambda X)\leq 2\exp\bigl\{(\sigma\lambda)^{r/(r-1)}\bigr\},

for all
\lambda>0
.

Proof.

Young’s inequality states that whenever
p,q>1
are such that
1/p+1/q=1
, we have
ab\leq a^{p}/p+b^{q}/q
for all
a,b\geq 0
. Hence

\displaystyle\lambda X\leq\lambda|X|\leq\frac{|X|^{r}}{r\sigma^{r}}+\frac{(\sigma\lambda)^{r/(r-1)}}{r/(r-1)}\leq\frac{|X|^{r}}{\sigma^{r}}+(\sigma\lambda)^{r/(r-1)}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\exp(\lambda X)\leq\mathbb{E}\bigl\{\exp(|X|^{r}/\sigma^{r})\bigr\}\cdot\exp\bigl\{(\sigma\lambda)^{r/(r-1)}\bigr\}\leq 2\exp\bigl\{(\sigma\lambda)^{r/(r-1)}\bigr\},

as required.
∎

Lemma 41

(PAC–Bayes lemma)
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a measurable space and let
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}P\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
. Let
\Xi\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\Xi)
. Further let
f:\mathcal{X}\times\Xi\to\mathbb{R}
be such that
\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P}(e^{f(X,\xi)})<\infty
for
\mu
-almost all
\xi\in\Xi
. Then, for every
\delta\in(0,1]
, we have with probability at least
1-\delta
that

\displaystyle\sup_{\rho\in\mathcal{P}(\Xi):\rho\ll\mu}\biggl\{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{\xi\sim\rho}f(X_{i},\xi)-\mathbb{E}_{\xi\sim\rho}\log\bigl\{\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P}(e^{f(X,\xi)})\bigr\}-\frac{\mathrm{KL}(\rho,\mu)+\log(1/\delta)}{n}\biggr\}\leq 0,

where, for instance,
\mathbb{E}_{\xi\sim\rho}f(X_{i},\xi)\coloneqq\int_{\Xi}f(X_{i},v)\,\mathrm{d}\rho(v)
.

Proof.

See, for example,
Zhivotovskiy 2024
.
∎

The following lemma provides a concentration result for the sample mean of independent and identically distributed sub-exponential random vectors. The proof strategy follows that of
Zhivotovskiy 2024
, who considered the case
n=1
.

Lemma 42
.

Let
\theta_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\Sigma\in\mathcal{S}_{++}^{d\times d}
,
\delta\in(0,1]
and
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}P\in\mathcal{P}_{d,\psi_{1}}(\theta_{0},\Sigma)
. Assume further that
\delta\geq 2e^{-n/3}
. Then with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\displaystyle\biggl\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}-\theta_{0}\biggr\|_{2}^{2}\leq 24\cdot\frac{\tr(\Sigma)+\|\Sigma\|_{\mathrm{op}}\log(2/\delta)}{n}.

Proof.

Let
\beta\coloneqq 2\log(2/\delta)
, let
\mu
denote the distribution of
\mathsf{N}_{d}(0,\beta^{-1}\Sigma)
and for
u\in\Sigma^{1/2}\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, let
\rho_{u}
denote the conditional distribution of
Y
given
\bigl\{\|Y-u\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{2\beta^{-1}\tr(\Sigma)}\bigr\}
, where
Y\sim\mathsf{N}_{d}(u,\beta^{-1}\Sigma)
. By the computation of
Zhivotovskiy 2024
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{KL}(\rho_{u},\mu)\leq\frac{\beta}{2}+\log 2\leq 2\log\Bigl(\frac{2}{\delta}\Bigr).

Now, let
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be such that
\|v-u\|_{2}\leq\sqrt{2\beta^{-1}\tr(\Sigma)}
, and for
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, define
f_{\lambda}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
by
f_{\lambda}(x,y)\coloneqq\lambda y^{\top}\Sigma^{-1/2}(x-\theta_{0})
. Then, for
X\sim P
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\bigl\|v^{\top}\Sigma^{-1/2}(X-\theta_{0})\bigr\|_{\psi_{1}}\leq\|v\|_{2}\leq\|\Sigma\|_{\mathrm{op}}^{1/2}+\sqrt{2\beta^{-1}\tr(\Sigma)}\eqqcolon R.

It follows by
Zhivotovskiy 2024
that

\displaystyle\log\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P}(e^{f_{\lambda}(X,v)})=\log\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P}(e^{\lambda\cdot v^{\top}\Sigma^{-1/2}(X-\theta_{0})})\leq 4\lambda^{2}R^{2},

for all
|\lambda|\leq\frac{1}{2R}
, so
\mathbb{E}_{\xi_{u}\sim\rho_{u}}\bigl\{\log\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P}(e^{f_{\lambda}(X,\xi_{u})})\bigr\}\leq 4\lambda^{2}R^{2}
for all
|\lambda|\leq\frac{1}{2R}
. The PAC–Bayes lemma (Lemma
41
) then yields that with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\displaystyle\sup_{u\in\Sigma^{1/2}\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\!\biggl\{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{\xi_{u}\sim\rho_{u}}f_{\lambda}(X_{i},\xi_{u})\!-\!\mathbb{E}_{\xi_{u}\sim\rho_{u}}\!\bigl\{\log\mathbb{E}_{X\sim P}(e^{f_{\lambda}(X,\xi_{u})})\bigr\}\!-\!\frac{\mathrm{KL}(\rho_{u},\mu)\!+\!\log(1/\delta)}{n}\biggr\}\leq 0.

Therefore, we deduce that with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\displaystyle\biggl\|\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{i}-\theta_{0}\biggr\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\sup_{u\in\Sigma^{1/2}\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}u^{\top}\Sigma^{-1/2}(X_{i}-\theta_{0})

\displaystyle=\sup_{u\in\Sigma^{1/2}\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\frac{1}{n\lambda}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{\xi_{u}\sim\rho_{u}}f_{\lambda}(X_{i},\xi_{u})

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{\lambda\in[0,\frac{1}{2R}]}\biggl\{4\lambda R^{2}+\frac{3\log(2/\delta)}{n\lambda}\biggr\}

\displaystyle{=}2R\sqrt{\frac{3\log(2/\delta)}{n}}=2\sqrt{\frac{3}{n}}\cdot\Bigl\{\sqrt{\tr(\Sigma)}+\sqrt{\|\Sigma\|_{\mathrm{op}}\log(2/\delta)}\Bigr\}.

where
(i)
follows by choosing
\lambda=\frac{1}{2R}\sqrt{\frac{3\log(2/\delta)}{n}}
, which is at most
\frac{1}{2R}
since
\frac{3\log(2/\delta)}{n}\leq 1
by assumption. The final conclusion follows by squaring both sides of the inequality above and using the inequality
(a+b)^{2}\leq 2a^{2}+2b^{2}
for
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
.
∎

Appendix G
Background on disintegrations

Our definition of MAR relies on the decomposition of a probability measure on a product space into the marginal distribution on one coordinate and a family of conditional distributions on the other. This can be achieved via the notion of disintegration. Let
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A})
and
(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B})
be measurable spaces, and let
P
be a probability measure on the product space
(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})
. Further, let
\mu
denote the marginal distribution of
P
on
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A})
. We say that
(P_{x})_{x\in\mathcal{X}}
is a
disintegration of
P
into conditional distributions on
\mathcal{Y}
if

(a)

P_{x}
is a probability measure on
(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B})
, for each
x\in\mathcal{X}
;

(b)

x\mapsto P_{x}(B)
is an
\mathcal{A}
-measurable function, for every
B\in\mathcal{B}
;

(c)

P(A\times B)=\int_{A}P_{x}(B)\,d\mu(x)
for all
A\in\mathcal{A}
and
B\in\mathcal{B}
.

In our setting,
P
denotes the joint distribution of a random pair
(X,Y)
, taking values in
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
respectively. We interpret
P_{x}
as the conditional distribution of
Y
given
X=x
, even though it may be the case that the conditioning event has probability zero. Going further, we also interpret
P_{X}
as the conditional distribution of
Y
given
X
. Indeed, we then have for all
A\in\mathcal{A}
and
B\in\mathcal{B}
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl(P_{X}(B)\mathbbm{1}_{A}(X)\bigr)=\int_{A}P_{x}(B)\,d\mu(x)=P(A\times B)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(X\in A,Y\in B)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigl(\mathbbm{1}_{A}(X)\mathbbm{1}_{B}(Y)\bigr),

so
\mathbb{P}(Y\in B\,|\,X)=\mathbb{E}\bigl(\mathbbm{1}_{B}(Y)\,|\,X)=P_{X}(B)
almost surely.
The following result, which follows from
Dudley 2018
, provides a sufficient condition for the existence of a disintegration and may be regarded as a generalisation of Fubini’s theorem for probability measures on the product of Polish spaces.

Theorem 43
.

Suppose that
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A})
and
(\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B})
are Polish spaces with their corresponding Borel
\sigma
-algebras. Let
P
be a probability distribution on
(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{B})
, with
\mu
denoting the marginal distribution of
P
on
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A})
. Then there exists a disintegration
(P_{x})_{x\in\mathcal{X}}
of
P
into conditional distributions on
\mathcal{Y}
with the property that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}}g(x,y)\,dP(x,y)=\int_{\mathcal{X}}\biggl(\int_{\mathcal{Y}}g(x,y)\,dP_{x}(y)\biggr)\,d\mu(x),

for every
P
-integrable function
g:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
. Moreover, the disintegration
(P_{x})_{x\in\mathcal{X}}
of
P
is unique in the sense that if there exists another disintegration
(\widetilde{P}_{x})_{x\in\mathcal{X}}
of
P
into conditional distributions on
\mathcal{Y}
, then
\widetilde{P}_{x}=P_{x}
for
\mu
-almost every
x\in\mathcal{X}
.

In order to apply this result in our missing data context, recall the random pair
(X,\Omega^{\prime})
taking values in
\mathcal{X}\times\{0,1\}^{d}
from (
2.1
). For each
\omega\in\{0,1\}^{d}
, we assume the existence of disintegrations
(P_{x\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}\omega})_{x\in\mathcal{X}}
of the joint distribution of
(X\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}\omega,\Omega^{\prime})
into conditional distributions on
\{0,1\}^{d}
as well as
(P_{x})_{x\in\mathcal{X}}
of the joint distribution of
(X,\Omega^{\prime})
into conditional distributions on
\{0,1\}^{d}
. The existence of these disintegrations is guaranteed by Theorem
43
when
\mathcal{X}_{j}
is a Polish space for each
j\in[d]
, because it then follows from Lemma
37
and its proof that
\mathcal{X}\coloneqq\prod_{j=1}^{d}\mathcal{X}_{j}
and
\mathcal{X}_{\star}\coloneqq\prod_{j=1}^{d}\mathcal{X}_{j,\star}
are Polish. Formally then, the condition
\mathbb{P}(\Omega^{\prime}=\omega\,|\,X=x)=\mathbb{P}(\Omega^{\prime}=\omega\,|\,X\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}\omega=x\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}\omega)
in (
2.1
) means that
P_{x}(\omega)=P_{x\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}\omega}(\omega)
. In fact, since the MAR definition refers to a family of distributions of
X\mathbin{\mathchoice{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\displaystyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\displaystyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\displaystyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\textstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\textstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\textstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptstyle\star$\cr}}}{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{0.77778}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bigcirc$}}}$\cr$\scriptscriptstyle\star$\cr}}}}\Omega^{\prime}
, we need these disintegrations for each possible joint distribution of
(X,\Omega^{\prime})
with
X\sim P
and
\mathbb{P}(\Omega^{\prime}=\bm{1}_{S})=\pi(S)
for all
S\subseteq[d]
(such disintegrations are again guaranteed to exist by Theorem
43
when
\mathcal{X}_{j}
is a Polish space for each
j\in[d]
).

Appendix H
MCAR lower bounds for mean estimation

Recall the definition of an
f
-divergence
\mathrm{Div}_{f}(\cdot,\cdot)
from (
80
). Lemma
45
below relates the
f
-divergence of two MCAR distributions on
\mathcal{X}_{\star}
to a notion of average
f
-divergence given in Definition
44
below. For probability measures
P,Q\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
, we let, for
S\subseteq[d]
,
P_{S}
and
Q_{S}
denote their respective marginal distributions on
\mathcal{X}_{S}
.

Definition 44
.

Let
P,Q\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
, let
\pi\in\mathcal{P}(2^{[d]})
and let
f:(0,\infty)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a convex function with
f(1)=0
. We define the
average
f
-divergence
between
P
and
Q
with respect to
\pi
to be

\displaystyle\mathrm{ADiv}_{f}(P,Q;\pi)\coloneqq\sum_{S\subseteq[d]}\pi(S)\cdot\mathrm{Div}_{f}\bigl(P_{S},Q_{S}\bigr),

where we adopt the convention that
\mathrm{Div}_{f}(P_{S},Q_{S})\coloneqq 0
if
S=\emptyset
.

We will write
\mathrm{ATV}(\cdot,\cdot;\pi)
and
\mathrm{AKL}(\cdot,\cdot;\pi)
respectively for the average total variation distance and average Kullback–Leibler divergence with respect to
\pi
. It is worth noting that the average total variation distance is a pseudo-metric but not necessarily a metric on
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
; indeed, we have
\mathrm{ATV}(P,Q;\pi)=0
whenever
P
and
Q
have the same marginal distributions on the support of
\pi
.

The following lemma shows how an
f
-divergence between two MCAR distributions on
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X}_{\star})
can be computed as an average
f
-divergence on
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
in the sense of Definition
44
.

Lemma 45
.

Let
P,Q\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})
and let
\pi\in\mathcal{P}(2^{[d]})
. Then

\mathrm{Div}_{f}\bigl(\mathsf{MCAR}_{(\pi,P)},\mathsf{MCAR}_{(\pi,Q)}\bigr)=\mathrm{ADiv}_{f}(P,Q;\pi).

Proof of Lemma
45
.

Recall the definition of
\mathcal{X}^{(S)}
and
\mathcal{X}_{S}
from Section
A
. For
A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{X}_{\star})
, note that
A\cap\mathcal{X}^{(S)}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{X}^{(S)})
and define
(A\cap\mathcal{X}^{(S)})_{S}\coloneqq\bigl\{x_{S}:x\in A\cap\mathcal{X}^{(S)}\bigr\}\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{X}_{S})
. Let
P^{(S)}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X}_{\star})
be defined as
P^{(S)}(A)\coloneqq P_{S}\bigl((A\cap\mathcal{X}^{(S)})_{S}\bigr)
for
A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{X}_{\star})
, so that
P^{(S)}
is supported on
\mathcal{X}^{(S)}
.
For each
S\subseteq[d]
, we can apply the Lebesgue decomposition theorem to obtain the decomposition
P^{(S)}=P^{(S)}_{\mathrm{ac}}+P^{(S)}_{\mathrm{sing}}
(with respect to
Q^{(S)}
). Then, with respect to
\mathsf{MCAR}_{(\pi,Q)}
,

\bigl(\mathsf{MCAR}_{(\pi,P)}\bigr)_{\mathrm{ac}}=\biggl(\sum_{S\subseteq[d]}\pi(S)\cdot P^{(S)}\biggr)_{\mathrm{ac}}=\sum_{S\subseteq[d]}\pi(S)\cdot P^{(S)}_{\mathrm{ac}}

and

\bigl(\mathsf{MCAR}_{(\pi,P)}\bigr)_{\mathrm{sing}}=\biggl(\sum_{S\subseteq[d]}\pi(S)\cdot P^{(S)}\biggr)_{\mathrm{sing}}=\sum_{S\subseteq[d]}\pi(S)\cdot P^{(S)}_{\mathrm{sing}}.

Hence, since
\mathcal{X}_{\star}=\sqcup_{S\subseteq[d]}\mathcal{X}^{(S)}
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{Div}_{f}

\displaystyle\bigl(\mathsf{MCAR}_{(\pi,P)},\mathsf{MCAR}_{(\pi,Q)}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{X}_{\star}}f\biggl(\frac{\mathrm{d}\sum_{S\subseteq[d]}\pi(S)P^{(S)}_{\mathrm{ac}}}{\mathrm{d}\sum_{S\subseteq[d]}\pi(S)Q^{(S)}}\biggr)\sum_{S\subseteq[d]}\pi(S)\,\mathrm{d}Q^{(S)}+M_{f}\cdot\sum_{S\subseteq[d]}\pi(S)P^{(S)}_{\mathrm{sing}}(\mathcal{X}_{\star})

\displaystyle=\sum_{S\subseteq[d]}\pi(S)\int_{\mathcal{X}^{(S)}}f\biggl(\frac{\mathrm{d}P^{(S)}_{\mathrm{ac}}}{\mathrm{d}Q^{(S)}}\biggr)\,\mathrm{d}Q^{(S)}+M_{f}\cdot\sum_{S\subseteq[d]}\pi(S)P^{(S)}_{\mathrm{sing}}(\mathcal{X}^{(S)})=\mathrm{ADiv}_{f}(P,Q;\pi),

as desired.
∎

Very often, it is convenient to apply Pinsker’s inequality to total variation distances, in order to control them via (more tractable) Kullback–Leibler divergences. We remark that in doing so directly to the left-hand side of Lemma
45
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}\bigl(\mathsf{MCAR}_{(\pi,P)},\mathsf{MCAR}_{(\pi,Q)}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2^{1/2}}\cdot\mathrm{KL}^{1/2}\bigl(\mathsf{MCAR}_{(\pi,P)},\mathsf{MCAR}_{(\pi,Q)}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{1/2}}\mathrm{AKL}^{1/2}(P,Q;\pi)=\frac{1}{2^{1/2}}\biggl\{\sum_{S\subseteq[d]}\pi(S)\cdot\mathrm{KL}\bigl(P_{S},Q_{S}\bigr)\biggr\}^{1/2}.

On the other hand, applying Pinsker’s inequality to the right-hand side of Lemma
45
yields the bound

\displaystyle\mathrm{ATV}(P,Q;\pi)=\sum_{S\subseteq[d]}\pi(S)\cdot\mathrm{TV}\bigl(P_{S},Q_{S}\bigr)\leq\frac{1}{2^{1/2}}\sum_{S\subseteq[d]}\pi(S)\cdot\mathrm{KL}^{1/2}\bigl(P_{S},Q_{S}\bigr),

which is an improvement, by Jensen’s inequality.

We now state two lower bounds in the MCAR setting, beginning with the univariate setting.

Proposition 46
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
q\in(0,1]
and
\Theta\coloneqq\mathbb{R}
.

(a)

Let
\sigma>0
and
\delta\in(0,1/4]
. Then, writing
\mathcal{P}_{\theta}\coloneqq\bigl\{\mathsf{MCAR}_{(q,\mathsf{N}(\theta,\sigma^{2}))}^{\otimes n}\bigr\}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}\bigl(\delta,\mathcal{P}_{\Theta},|\cdot|^{2}\bigr)\begin{cases}\geq\dfrac{\sigma^{2}\log(1/\delta)}{20nq}\quad&\text{if }\delta\geq\dfrac{(1-q)^{n}}{2}\\
=\infty\quad&\text{if }\delta<\dfrac{(1-q)^{n}}{2}.\end{cases}

(b)

Let
K>0
and
\delta\in(0,1/4]
. Then, with
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{b}}(\theta,K)
as in (
133
) and writing
\mathcal{P}_{\theta}\coloneqq\bigl\{\mathsf{MCAR}_{(q,P)}^{\otimes n}:P\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{b}}(\theta,K)\bigr\}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}\bigl(\delta,\mathcal{P}_{\Theta},|\cdot|^{2}\bigr)\begin{cases}\geq\dfrac{K^{2}\log(1/\delta)}{80nq}\quad&\text{if }\delta\geq\exp(-nq/2)\\
=\infty\quad&\text{if }\delta<\dfrac{(1-q)^{n}}{2}.\end{cases}

Proof.

(a)
Let
\theta_{1}\coloneqq 0
and
\theta_{2}\coloneqq\sigma\sqrt{\frac{1}{nq}\log\bigl(\frac{1}{4\delta(1-\delta)}\bigr)}
. By Lemma
45
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{KL}\bigl(\mathsf{MCAR}^{\otimes n}_{(\pi,\mathsf{N}(\theta_{1},\sigma^{2}))},\mathsf{MCAR}^{\otimes n}_{(\pi,\mathsf{N}(\theta_{2},\sigma^{2}))}\bigr)

\displaystyle=nq\cdot\mathrm{KL}\bigl(\mathsf{N}(\theta_{1},\sigma^{2}),\mathsf{N}(\theta_{2},\sigma^{2})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\log\biggl(\frac{1}{4\delta(1-\delta)}\biggr)<\log\biggl(\frac{1}{4\delta(1-\delta)}\biggr).

Therefore, by
Ma, Verchand and Samworth 2024
, we deduce that for
\delta\in(0,1/4]
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}\bigl(\delta,\mathcal{P}_{\Theta},|\cdot|^{2}\bigr)\geq\biggl(\frac{\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}}{2}\biggr)^{2}=\frac{\sigma^{2}\log\bigl(\frac{1}{4\delta(1-\delta)}\bigr)}{4nq}\geq\frac{\sigma^{2}\log(1/\delta)}{20nq}.

Moreover, for any
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}\bigl(

\displaystyle\mathsf{MCAR}_{(q,\mathsf{N}(\theta_{1},\sigma^{2}))}^{\otimes n},\mathsf{MCAR}_{(q,\mathsf{N}(\theta_{2},\sigma^{2}))}^{\otimes n}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\sup_{A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}_{\star}^{n})\setminus\{\star\}^{n}}\Bigl\{\mathsf{MCAR}_{(q,\mathsf{N}(\theta_{1},\sigma^{2}))}^{\otimes n}(A)-\mathsf{MCAR}_{(q,\mathsf{N}(\theta_{2},\sigma^{2}))}^{\otimes n}(A)\Bigr\}\leq 1-(1-q)^{n},

where both steps follow since
\mathsf{MCAR}_{(q,\mathsf{N}(\theta_{1},\sigma^{2}))}^{\otimes n}(\{\star\}^{n})=\mathsf{MCAR}_{(q,\mathsf{N}(\theta_{2},\sigma^{2}))}^{\otimes n}(\{\star\}^{n})=(1-q)^{n}
. Therefore, by
Ma, Verchand and Samworth 2024
, we have that
\mathcal{M}(\delta,\mathcal{P}_{\Theta},|\cdot|^{2})\geq(\theta_{1}-\theta_{2})^{2}/4
for
\delta<\frac{(1-q)^{n}}{2}
. The claim follows since
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}
were arbitrary.

(b)
Define
P_{1},P_{2}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R})
by

P_{1}(\{x\})\coloneqq\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2}\quad&\text{ if }x=0\\
\frac{1}{2}\quad&\text{ if }x=K\end{cases}\quad\text{and}\quad P_{2}(\{x\})\coloneqq\begin{cases}\frac{1-a}{2}\quad&\text{ if }x=0\\
\frac{1+a}{2}\quad&\text{ if }x=K,\end{cases}

where
a\coloneqq\sqrt{\frac{1}{nq}\log\bigl(\frac{1}{4\delta(1-\delta)}\bigr)}\leq\sqrt{\frac{\log(1/\delta)}{nq}}\leq 1/\sqrt{2}
for
\delta\in[e^{-nq/2},1/4]
.
Let
\theta_{1}\coloneqq\mathbb{E}_{P_{1}}(X)=K/2
and
\theta_{2}\coloneqq\mathbb{E}_{P_{2}}(X)=(1+a)K/2
, so that
P_{\ell}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{b}}(\theta_{\ell},K)
for
\ell\in\{1,2\}
. Moreover, by Lemma
45
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{KL}\bigl(\mathsf{MCAR}^{\otimes n}_{(q,P_{1})},\mathsf{MCAR}^{\otimes n}_{(q,P_{2})}\bigr)=nq\mathrm{KL}(P_{1},P_{2})=\frac{nq}{2}\log\biggl(\frac{1}{1-a^{2}}\biggr)<nqa^{2}=\log\biggl(\frac{1}{4\delta(1-\delta)}\biggr),

where the inequality follows because

\log\bigl(\frac{1}{1-x^{2}}\bigr)<2x^{2}
for
x\in(0,1/\sqrt{2}]
. Hence, by
Ma, Verchand and Samworth 2024
, we deduce that for
\delta\in[e^{-nq/2},1/4]
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}\bigl(\delta,\mathcal{P}_{\Theta},|\cdot|^{2}\bigr)\geq\biggl(\frac{\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}}{2}\biggr)^{2}\geq\frac{K^{2}\log(1/\delta)}{80nq}.

Now let
\theta\in\mathbb{R}
,
P_{1}^{\prime}\coloneqq\mathsf{Unif}[0,K]
and
P_{2}^{\prime}\coloneqq\mathsf{Unif}[\theta,\theta+K]
. Then by the same argument as in part
(a)
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}\bigl(\mathsf{MCAR}_{(q,P_{1}^{\prime})}^{\otimes n},\mathsf{MCAR}_{(q,P_{2}^{\prime})}^{\otimes n}\bigr)\leq 1-(1-q)^{n}.

Therefore, by
Ma, Verchand and Samworth 2024
, we have that
\mathcal{M}(\delta,\mathcal{P}_{\Theta},|\cdot|^{2})\geq\theta^{2}/4
for
\delta<\frac{(1-q)^{n}}{2}
. The claim follows since
\theta_{1},\theta_{2}
were arbitrary.
∎

Our next proposition lower bounds the minimax quantile for mean estimation in the multivariate Gaussian setting when the covariance matrix is diagonal.

Proposition 47
.

Let
\delta\in(0,1/4]
,
\Sigma=(\Sigma_{jk})_{j,k\in[d]}\in\mathcal{S}^{d\times d}_{++}
be diagonal,
\pi\in\mathcal{P}(2^{[d]})
, and let
P_{\theta}\coloneqq\mathsf{N}(\theta,\Sigma)
for
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then, writing
\mathcal{P}_{\theta}\coloneqq\bigl\{\mathsf{MCAR}_{(\pi,P_{\theta})}^{\otimes n}\bigr\}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}\bigl(\delta,\mathcal{P}_{\Theta},\|\cdot\|_{2}^{2}\bigr)\gtrsim\frac{\tr\bigl(\Sigma^{\mathrm{IPW}}\bigr)}{n}+\frac{\|\Sigma^{\mathrm{IPW}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\log(1/\delta)}{n}.

Proof.

We consider two separate constructions to capture each of the terms in the lower bound. For the first, let
\mathcal{V}\coloneqq\{0,1\}^{d}
and for each
v=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{d})^{\top}\in\mathcal{V}
, set
\theta_{v}=(\theta_{v,1},\ldots,\theta_{v,d})^{\top}\coloneqq a\odot v
, where
a=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{d})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is given by
a_{j}\coloneqq\frac{4}{3}\sqrt{\Sigma_{jj}/(nq_{j})}
for
j\in[d]
. Define
\Theta_{0}\coloneqq\{\theta_{v}:v\in\mathcal{V}\}
, which has diameter
D\coloneqq\frac{4}{3}\sqrt{\tr(\Sigma^{\mathrm{IPW}})/n}
. For any
v,v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}
that differ only in their
j
th coordinates, we have by Pinsker’s inequality and Lemma
45
that

\displaystyle\mathrm{TV}\bigl(\mathsf{MCAR}_{(\pi,P_{\theta_{v}})}^{\otimes n},\mathsf{MCAR}_{(\pi,P_{\theta_{v^{\prime}}})}^{\otimes n}\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\biggl\{\frac{n}{2}\cdot\mathrm{KL}\bigl(\mathsf{MCAR}_{(\pi,P_{\theta_{v}})},\mathsf{MCAR}_{(\pi,P_{\theta_{v^{\prime}}})}\bigr)\biggr\}^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\biggl\{\frac{n}{2}\sum_{S\subseteq[d]}\pi(S)\cdot\mathrm{KL}\bigl((P_{\theta_{v}})_{S},(P_{\theta_{v^{\prime}}})_{S}\bigr)\biggr\}^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\biggl\{\frac{n}{2}\sum_{S\subseteq[d]}\pi(S)\cdot\sum_{k\in S}\frac{(\theta_{v,k}-\theta_{v^{\prime},k})^{2}}{2\Sigma_{kk}}\biggr\}^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\biggl\{\frac{n}{4}\sum_{S\subseteq[d]:j\in S}\pi(S)\cdot\frac{a_{j}^{2}}{\Sigma_{jj}}\biggr\}^{1/2}=\frac{2}{3}.

Therefore, by Assouad’s Lemma
(e.g.,
Ma, Verchand and Samworth 2024
, Lemma 23)
,

\displaystyle\inf_{\widehat{\theta}_{n}\in\widehat{\Theta}_{n}}\sup_{\theta_{0}\in\Theta_{0}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{MCAR}_{(\pi,P_{\theta_{0}})}^{\otimes n}}\bigl(\|\widehat{\theta}_{n}-\theta\|_{2}^{2}\bigr)\geq\frac{4\tr(\Sigma^{\mathrm{IPW}})}{27n}.

Applying
Ma, Verchand and Samworth 2024
, with
\epsilon=3/40
therein, we deduce that for
\delta\in(0,1/15]
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{-}\bigl(\delta,\mathcal{P}_{\Theta},\|\cdot\|_{2}^{2}\bigr)\geq\frac{\tr(\Sigma^{\mathrm{IPW}})}{100n}.

We then apply
Ma, Verchand and Samworth 2024
, with
A=k=2
therein, to deduce that for
\delta\in(0,1/4]
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}\bigl(\delta,\mathcal{P}_{\Theta},\|\cdot\|_{2}^{2}\bigr)\geq\frac{\tr(\Sigma^{\mathrm{IPW}})}{2^{6}\cdot 3^{2}\cdot 5^{2}\cdot n}.

(149)

Our second construction involves just two distributions. Let
j_{0}\coloneqq\sargmax_{j\in[d]}\Sigma_{jj}/q_{j}
and set
\theta_{1}\coloneqq 0
,
\theta_{2}\coloneqq\sqrt{\frac{\Sigma_{j_{0}j_{0}}}{nq_{j_{0}}}\log\bigl(\frac{1}{4\delta(1-\delta)}\bigr)}\,e_{j_{0}}
. Then by Lemma
45
,

\mathrm{KL}\bigl(\mathsf{MCAR}_{(\pi,P_{\theta_{1}})}^{\otimes n},\mathsf{MCAR}_{(\pi,P_{\theta_{2}})}^{\otimes n}\bigr)=n\cdot\mathrm{AKL}\bigl(P_{\theta_{1}},P_{\theta_{2}};\pi\bigr)=\frac{1}{2}\log\biggl(\frac{1}{4\delta(1-\delta)}\biggr).

By
Ma, Verchand and Samworth 2024
, we have for
\delta\in(0,1/4]
that

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}\bigl(\delta,\mathcal{P}_{\Theta},\|\cdot\|_{2}^{2}\bigr)\geq\frac{\|\Sigma^{\mathrm{IPW}}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\log(1/\delta)}{20n}.

(150)

Finally, combining (
149
) and (
150
) yields the desired result.
∎

Appendix I
Robust mean estimation algorithms for completely observed data

In this section, we briefly review some of the robust mean estimators.

I.1
Median of means

Given a sequence of real numbers
(a_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
, let
a_{(1)}\leq a_{(2)}\leq\cdots\leq a_{(n)}
be the sorted version of
(a_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
. Then the median of
(a_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
is defined as
\mathrm{Median}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\coloneqq a_{(\lfloor n/2\rfloor+1)}
.

Algorithm 2

Median of means

Input:
Data
(x_{i})_{i\in[n]}\in\mathbb{R}
and the number of blocks
M\in\mathbb{N}

Output:

\widehat{\theta}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}

1:

function

Median_of_Means
(
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n};M
)

2:

Randomly partition
[n]
into
M
disjoint subsets
(B_{m})_{m=1}^{M}
such that

\lfloor n/M\rfloor\leq|B_{m}|\leq\lceil n/M\rceil
for all
m\in[M]

3:

for

m\in[M]

do

4:

\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\hskip 1.29167pt\hskip 0.0pt\rule[5.59721pt]{4.2024pt}{0.43057pt}}{x}_{m}\leftarrow\frac{1}{|B_{m}|}\sum_{i\in B_{m}}x_{i}

5:

end

for

6:

\widehat{\theta}_{n}\leftarrow\mathrm{Median}(\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\hskip 1.29167pt\hskip 0.0pt\rule[5.59721pt]{4.2024pt}{0.43057pt}}{x}_{1},\ldots,\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\hskip 1.29167pt\hskip 0.0pt\rule[5.59721pt]{4.2024pt}{0.43057pt}}{x}_{M})

7:

return

\widehat{\theta}_{n}

8:

end

function

Lemma 48

(
Lerasle and Oliveira 2011
, Proposition 1)
.

Let
X_{1},\ldots,X_{n}\overset{\mathrm{iid}}{\sim}P\in\mathcal{P}_{L^{2}}(\theta_{0},\sigma^{2})
,
\delta\in\bigl[\exp(1-n/2),1\bigr]
,
M\coloneqq\lceil\log(1/\delta)\rceil
and
\widehat{\theta}_{n}\coloneqq\textsc{Median\_of\_Means}(X_{1},\ldots,X_{n};M)
. Then with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\displaystyle\bigl(\widehat{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{0}\bigr)^{2}\leq\frac{24e\sigma^{2}\log(e/\delta)}{n}.

I.2
Univariate trimmed mean

For
\alpha\leq\beta\in\mathbb{R}
, we define
T_{\alpha,\beta}:\mathbb{R}\to[\alpha,\beta]
by

\displaystyle T_{\alpha,\beta}(x)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\beta\quad&\text{if }x\geq\beta\\
x&\text{if }\alpha<x<\beta\\
\alpha&\text{if }x\leq\alpha.\end{cases}

The univariate trimmed mean estimator is defined in Algorithm
3
.

Algorithm 3

Univariate trimmed mean

Input:
Data
(x_{i})_{i\in[n]}\in\mathbb{R}
, contamination parameter
\epsilon\in[0,1)
, and tolerance parameter
\delta\in(0,1]

Output:

\widehat{\theta}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}

1:

function

Univariate_Trimmed_Mean
(
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n};\epsilon,\delta
)

2:

Randomly partition
\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}
into two disjoint sets
\{y_{1},\ldots,y_{n/2}\}
and

\{z_{1},\ldots,z_{n/2}\}

3:

Arrange
\{z_{1},\ldots,z_{n/2}\}
in increasing order
\widetilde{z}_{1}\leq\cdots\leq\widetilde{z}_{n/2}

4:

\eta\leftarrow 8\epsilon+24\log(4/\delta)/n

5:

\alpha\leftarrow\widetilde{z}_{n\eta/2}
,
\beta\leftarrow\widetilde{z}_{n(1-\eta)/2}

6:

\widehat{\theta}_{n}\leftarrow\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n/2}T_{\alpha,\beta}(y_{i})

7:

return

\widehat{\theta}_{n}

8:

end

function

I.3
Robust Descent

Algorithms
4
and
5
below provide pseudo-code for the robust (block) descent algorithm of
Depersin and Lecué 2022b
. To describe the
Solve_SDP
function in line
7
of Algorithm
4
, define

\Delta_{M}\coloneqq\biggl\{(w_{m})_{m=1}^{M}:0\leq w_{m}\leq\frac{10}{9M},\,\sum_{m=1}^{M}w_{m}=1\biggr\},

and let
\mathcal{V}\coloneqq\{V\in\mathcal{S}^{d\times d}_{+}:\tr(V)=1\}
. The
\textsc{Solve\_SDP}(\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\hskip 1.29167pt\hskip 0.0pt\rule[5.59721pt]{4.2024pt}{0.43057pt}}{x}_{1},\ldots,\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\hskip 1.29167pt\hskip 0.0pt\rule[5.59721pt]{4.2024pt}{0.43057pt}}{x}_{M};\theta)
function in line
7
of Algorithm
4
provides an approximate maximiser
\widehat{V}\in\mathcal{V}
of the function
h_{\theta}:\mathcal{V}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
given by

\displaystyle h_{\theta}(V)\coloneqq\min_{(w_{1},\ldots,w_{m})\in\Delta_{M}}\tr\biggl\{V^{\top}\sum_{m=1}^{M}w_{m}\bigl(\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\hskip 1.29167pt\hskip 0.0pt\rule[5.59721pt]{4.2024pt}{0.43057pt}}{x}_{m}-\theta\bigr)\bigl(\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\hskip 1.29167pt\hskip 0.0pt\rule[5.59721pt]{4.2024pt}{0.43057pt}}{x}_{m}-\theta\bigr)^{\top}\biggr\}.

(151)

A full description of
Solve_SDP
can be found in
Depersin and Lecué 2022b
; see in particular their Algorithms 2 and 3.

In Algorithm
4
below, we invoke the notation that if
S\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
is compact and
f:S\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is continuous, then
\sargmax_{x\in S}f(x)
denotes the smallest element of the argmax set in the lexicographic ordering.

Algorithm 4

Robust Block Descent

Input:

\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\hskip 1.29167pt\hskip 0.0pt\rule[5.59721pt]{4.2024pt}{0.43057pt}}{x}_{1},\ldots,\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\hskip 1.29167pt\hskip 0.0pt\rule[5.59721pt]{4.2024pt}{0.43057pt}}{x}_{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

Output:

\widehat{\theta}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

1:

function

Robust_Block_Descent
(
\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\hskip 1.29167pt\hskip 0.0pt\rule[5.59721pt]{4.2024pt}{0.43057pt}}{x}_{1},\ldots,\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\hskip 1.29167pt\hskip 0.0pt\rule[5.59721pt]{4.2024pt}{0.43057pt}}{x}_{M}
)

2:

T\leftarrow\lceil\log(8\sqrt{d})/\log(10/9)\rceil

3:

for

j\in[d]

do

4:

\widehat{\theta}^{(0)}_{j}\leftarrow\mathrm{Median}(\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\hskip 1.29167pt\hskip 0.0pt\rule[5.59721pt]{4.2024pt}{0.43057pt}}{x}_{1j},\ldots,\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\hskip 1.29167pt\hskip 0.0pt\rule[5.59721pt]{4.2024pt}{0.43057pt}}{x}_{Mj})

5:

end

for

6:

for

t\in[T]

do

7:

\widehat{V}^{(t)}\leftarrow\textsc{Solve\_SDP}(\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\hskip 1.29167pt\hskip 0.0pt\rule[5.59721pt]{4.2024pt}{0.43057pt}}{x}_{1},\ldots,\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\hskip 1.29167pt\hskip 0.0pt\rule[5.59721pt]{4.2024pt}{0.43057pt}}{x}_{M};\widehat{\theta}^{(t-1)})
; see (
151
)

8:

\widehat{v}^{(t)}\leftarrow\sargmax_{u\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}u^{\top}\widehat{V}^{(t)}u

9:

s^{(t)}\leftarrow-\mathrm{Median}\bigl((\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\hskip 1.29167pt\hskip 0.0pt\rule[5.59721pt]{4.2024pt}{0.43057pt}}{x}_{m}-\widehat{\theta}^{(t-1)})^{\top}\widehat{v}^{(t)}:m\in[M]\bigr)

10:

\widehat{\theta}^{(t)}\leftarrow\widehat{\theta}^{(t-1)}-s^{(t)}\widehat{v}^{(t)}

11:

end

for

12:

\widehat{\theta}_{n}\leftarrow\widehat{\theta}^{(T)}

13:

return

\widehat{\theta}_{n}

14:

end

function

Algorithm 5

Robust Descent

Input:
Data
(x_{i})_{i\in[n]}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, contamination parameter
\epsilon\in[0,1)
, and tolerance parameter
\delta\in(0,1]

Output:

\widehat{\theta}_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

1:

function

Robust_Descent
(
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n};\epsilon,\delta
)

2:

M\leftarrow\lceil 300\bigl(2\epsilon n+\log(2/\delta)\bigr)\vee 180{,}000\log(2/\delta)\rceil\wedge n

3:

Randomly draw
M
disjoint subsets
(B_{m})_{m=1}^{M}
from
[n]
, each with size
\lfloor n/M\rfloor

4:

for

m\in[M]

do

5:

\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\hskip 1.29167pt\hskip 0.0pt\rule[5.59721pt]{4.2024pt}{0.43057pt}}{x}_{m}\leftarrow|B_{m}|^{-1}\sum_{i\in B_{m}}x_{i}

6:

end

for

7:

\widehat{\theta}_{n}\leftarrow\textsc{Robust\_Block\_Descent}(\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\hskip 1.29167pt\hskip 0.0pt\rule[5.59721pt]{4.2024pt}{0.43057pt}}{x}_{1},\ldots,\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\hskip 1.29167pt\hskip 0.0pt\rule[5.59721pt]{4.2024pt}{0.43057pt}}{x}_{M})

8:

return

\widehat{\theta}_{n}

9:

end

function
